=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a sequence A = (A_1, A_2, \dots, A_N) of length N.
Answer Q queries. The i-th query (1 \leq i \leq Q) is as follows:

- You are given integers R_i and X_i. Consider a subsequence (not necessarily contiguous) of (A_1, A_2, \dots, A_{R_i}) that is strictly increasing and consists only of elements at most X_i. Find the maximum possible length of such a subsequence.
It is guaranteed that X_i \geq \min\lbrace A_1, A_2,\dots,A_{R_i} \rbrace.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N Q
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N
R_1 X_1
R_2 X_2
\vdots
R_Q X_Q

Output

Print Q lines. The i-th line should contain the answer to the i-th query.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N,Q \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 10^9
- 1 \leq R_i \leq N
- \min\lbrace A_1, A_2,\dots,A_{R_i} \rbrace\leq X_i\leq 10^9
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5 3
2 4 1 3 3
2 5
5 2
5 3

Sample Output 1

2
1
2


- 1st query: For the sequence (2,4), the longest strictly increasing subsequence with all elements at most 5 has length 2. Specifically, (2,4) qualifies.
- 2nd query: For the sequence (2,4,1,3,3), the longest strictly increasing subsequence with all elements at most 2 has length 1. Specifically, (2) and (1) qualify.
- 3rd query: For the sequence (2,4,1,3,3), the longest strictly increasing subsequence with all elements at most 3 has length 2. Specifically, (2,3) and (1,3) qualify.

Sample Input 2

10 8
2 5 6 5 2 1 7 9 7 2
7 8
5 2
2 3
2 6
7 3
8 9
9 6
8 7

Sample Output 2

4
1
1
2
1
5
3
4


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's think about iterative lazy segment tree for range chmax point query:
We need apply update to O(log M) nodes covering range, and point query by taking max over nodes on path from leaf to root. This is like "segment tree beats" no. For range update and point query, we can use a segment tree where update range decomposes into nodes and sets tag[node] = max(tag[node], val). Point query starts at leaf index pos+M, ans=0; while idx>0: ans=max(ans, tag[idx]); idx//=2. This works for range update point query if update covers range by canonical nodes. Need implement iterative range update on segment tree with size n power of two.

Standard iterative segment tree range update for lazy tags:
def range_chmax(l,r,val): # inclusive? half-open [l,r)
    l += size; r += size
    while l < r:
        if l&1: tag[l] = max(tag[l], val); l += 1
        if r&1: r -= 1; tag[r] = max(tag[r], val)
        l //= 2; r //= 2
Point query:
idx = pos + size
res = 0
while idx:
    res = max(res, tag[idx])
    idx //= 2
This is correct because every update range is decomposed into nodes fully covered; point query visits ancestors of leaf, including those nodes. Need initialize tag all 0. Since values nonnegative. Good. This is O(log M) update and query. Very simple.

But careful: In iterative range update, tags on internal nodes may be overwritten? We take max, so no need push. If multiple updates, point query max over all tags on ancestors. This is standard for range add point query? For range add, tags sum; for range chmax, max. Since operation is max and point query max of tags, yes. Need no propagation because updates are "set tag at node to max"; a point query combines all tags along path. If a node's tag updated, all leaves under it get that value. If later a subrange update updates descendant, point query sees both. If later a larger range update updates ancestor, point query sees max. Good.

Need ensure range update [l, r) with l=pos_a, r=M. If M maybe not power of two. Use size = 1; while size < M: size*=2. Range update with l,r in [0,M). If r=M, okay. But iterative update uses leaf indices pos+size; internal nodes may cover beyond M? If r=M not size, decomposition won't update nodes that cover beyond M? It updates canonical nodes within [l,M), not beyond. Since r=size? If M not power of two, r=M not equal size. The iterative algorithm with r += size uses r = M+size, not necessarily size+size; it will decompose exactly [l, M) in the tree, not including leaves beyond M? Let's verify. Standard iterative range update works for half-open [l,r) with r <= n where n=size? Actually if n is size (power of two) and r <= n, it works. If M < size, we should pass r=M, not size, to avoid updating dummy leaves. But point query only for valid leaves. If update dummy leaves doesn't affect answers, but could affect internal nodes that also cover valid leaves? If we update [l, size) including dummy, it might set tags on ancestors that also cover valid leaves beyond M? But no query beyond M, and for valid leaves, if update [l,size) includes them, it's okay because threshold range should extend to infinity, including all valid coordinates. Updating dummy leaves doesn't matter. But if we use r=size, range [l,size) covers all valid leaves from l to M-1 and dummy. Tags on ancestors that cover both valid and dummy still apply to valid leaves; that's correct because update should apply to all valid leaves >=l. So we can simply use r=size (power-of-two size) for suffix update, not M. But if we include query X values? All valid leaves are within [0,M); updating beyond M harmless. However, if we update [l, size), point query at valid leaf sees tags on ancestors that may include dummy leaves; but the tag is applied to all leaves under that ancestor, including valid ones. Since update should apply to all valid leaves >=l, yes. If an ancestor covers some valid leaves <l and some >=l? Iterative decomposition ensures not? Wait if we update [l, size) with l not aligned, standard decomposition covers nodes exactly within [l,size), no node extends below l. So no valid leaf <l gets tag. Good. So using r=size is okay. But if we use r=M, also okay. Need ensure M maybe 0? N>=1 Q>=1 so M>=1.

Now, query F(a-1): Need get predecessor index < a. Since a in vals. We can use bisect_left(vals, a) - 1. If idx < 0, v=0. Else v = point_query(idx). Implementation plan:
import sys, bisect
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N,Q = map(int,input().split())
    A = list(map(int,input().split()))
    queries = []
    Xs = []
    for _ in range(Q):
        R,X = map(int,input().split())
        queries.append((R,X))
        Xs.append(X)
    vals = sorted(set(A + Xs))  # A+Xs creates new list; okay 4e5
    M = len(vals)
    size = 1
    while size < M: size <<= 1
    tag = [0]*(2*size)
    def range_chmax(l,r,val):
        # half-open [l,r), r can be M or size
        l += size; r += size
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                if tag[l] < val: tag[l] = val
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                if tag[r] < val: tag[r] = val
            l >>= 1; r >>= 1
    def point_query(p):
        p += size
        res = 0
        while p:
            if tag[p] > res: res = tag[p]
            p >>= 1
        return res
    # precompute posA
    posA = [bisect_left(vals, a) for a in A]  # or dict
    # bucket queries
    buckets = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]
    for qi,(R,X) in enumerate(queries):
        px = bisect_left(vals, X)
        buckets[R].append((px, qi))
    ans = [0]*Q
    for i,a in enumerate(A, start=1):
        p = posA[i-1]
        # v = F(a-1)
        if p == 0:
            v = 0
        else:
            v = point_query(p-1)  # predecessor index p-1? Wait if p is index of a, predecessor index p-1. But is p-1 always largest value <a? Since vals sorted unique and a in vals, yes. But if there are duplicate? unique. So no need bisect. However, if p=0 no predecessor.
        range_chmax(p, size, v+1)  # update suffix [p, size) all thresholds >=a
        for px, qi in buckets[i]:
            ans[qi] = point_query(px)
    print('\n'.join(map(str, ans)))

But is F(a-1) = point_query(p-1) always? Suppose p-1 is a query X that is less than a, but there might be an A value equal to a? no. Since p is first index with value a. All values <a are before p. The largest value <a is vals[p-1]. F(a-1) = F(vals[p-1]) if no A values in (vals[p-1], a). There are no values in vals in that interval by definition. Since all A values are in vals, no A in interval. Thus F constant. Good. We don't need bisect_left(a)-1 because p is that. But wait, if vals includes query X values, p-1 might be query X, not A. Let's attempt to derive recurrence more rigorously:
Let dp_i(x) = answer for prefix i with bound x.
For new element a=A_i:
If a > x, dp_i(x)=dp_{i-1}(x).
If a <= x, any optimal subsequence either excludes i -> dp_{i-1}(x), or includes i. If includes i, the preceding subsequence is from prefix i-1, strictly increasing, all values < a (because to append a and maintain strict increase, last previous < a; since increasing, all previous < last < a). Thus its length is at most dp_{i-1}(a-1). Conversely, take an optimal subsequence for dp_{i-1}(a-1); all its values <= a-1, so append a yields valid. Hence dp_i(x)=max(dp_{i-1}(x), dp_{i-1}(a-1)+1) for x>=a.
So update is suffix chmax.

Now, coordinate compression:
We only need dp at values in S = {A_i} ∪ {X_i}. For a, dp(a-1) = dp(largest S value < a), because no update threshold lies between. Since dp is nondecreasing. We can maintain dp at compressed indices. Range update from index of a to end.

Now, data structure:
Segment tree with range chmax and point query. Actually, because updates are suffix chmax, we could use Fenwick tree for prefix max? Wait update suffix chmax and point query. If we reverse indices, update prefix chmax and point query. A Fenwick tree can support range chmax update and point query? Let's see: update [l, M) with max c. Point query at p: max over updates with l <= p. If we store at index l the value c, then point query p = max over l<=p of c. This is prefix maximum query over update starts! Ah! Since updates are suffix [l, end), a point p is covered iff update start l <= p. So we can simply maintain an array best_start[l] = max(best_start[l], c), and point_query(p) = max_{l<=p} best_start[l]. This is a Fenwick tree for prefix max with point update at l and prefix query. Wait, is that sufficient? Let's examine.

Each update: for all thresholds index >= p, F(index) = max(F(index), c). This is equivalent to adding a "tag" at start p with value c; any point q >= p receives it. To answer F(q), take max over all updates with start <= q. Since updates are suffix, yes. We don't need segment tree range update; we can do:
- update at position p: bit_update(p, c) where Fenwick stores max at index p? Actually we need max over starts <= q. If we have array start_max[p] = max c among updates starting at p. Then F(q)=max_{p<=q} start_max[p]. This is prefix max. Fenwick tree can support point update (set start_max[p] = max(start_max[p], c)) and prefix query max over [0..q]. Since point update at p and prefix query. But wait, if multiple updates start at same p, take max. Fenwick for prefix max with point update: update index p (1-based) with value c by while i<=M: tree[i]=max(tree[i], c); i+=i&-i. Query prefix q: res=max over tree while i>0. This gives max over all point updates at indices <=q. Yes! This is even simpler O(log M) and no need range segment tree. But is F(q) exactly prefix max of update starts? Let's verify with recurrence: Each update is suffix chmax with start p. The current F at q is max of all update values whose start <= q. Because every update applies to all positions >= start. Since updates are only suffix, the set of updates covering q are those with start <= q. The max over those starts is prefix max. So yes! We don't need segment tree at all. Nice.

Let's test: Initially no updates. For each A_i at p, v = F(a-1) = point_query(p-1) (prefix max over starts <= p-1). Then update start p with c=v+1: point_update(p, c). Then F(q) = prefix_max(q). Wait, is F(q) after update equal to prefix_max(q) over all updates? Let's prove by induction:
Suppose before update, F_old(q) = max_{start <= q} U[start] for all q, where U[start] is max c of updates starting at start. New update at p with c. For q < p, unchanged, prefix max over starts <=q unchanged because new start p>q. For q >= p, new F(q)=max(F_old(q), c)=max(prefix_old(q), c). New prefix max over starts <=q = max(prefix_old(q), max_{starts <=q, including p} U_new) = max(prefix_old(q), c) because c at p<=q. So matches. Great. Thus we can use Fenwick tree for prefix max with point updates. This is much simpler and faster.

- vals indices 0..M-1.

- prefix_query(pos0): i=pos0+1; res=0; while i>0: res=max(res, tree[i]); i -= i&-i. This returns max over starts <= pos0. If pos0=-1, return 0.

Thus final solution can be described as Fenwick tree over compressed values:
- Coordinate compress all A_i and query X_i.
- For each position i:
  - p = index of A_i.
  - best = bit.query(p)??? Need careful 1-based. If p is 0-based index, values <a are indices 0..p-1. Fenwick prefix query up to p (1-based) returns max over indices <= p-1? Let's define Fenwick 1-based where update at index p+1. Query(k) returns max over first k 1-based indices, i.e., 0-based indices 0..k-1. So to query values <a (0..p-1), call query(p) (since p = number of indices before p). If p=0, query(0)=0.
  - new_len = best + 1.
  - update(p+1, new_len) (point update at value index p).
- For query X with compressed index px (0-based), answer = query(px+1) (max over indices 0..px).

This is even simpler. Let's map:
Fenwick tree size M.
update(pos1, val): pos1 is 1-based index of value. while pos1 <= M: tree[pos1] = max(tree[pos1], val); pos1 += pos1 & -pos1.
query(pos1): returns max over [1..pos1]. If pos1=0 return 0.
For A_i p0:
best = query(p0)  # because p0 = number of compressed values less than a; 1-based indices 1..p0 correspond to 0-based 0..p0-1.
update(p0+1, best+1)
For query X px0:
ans = query(px0+1)

This is essentially Fenwick for LIS with bounded values. Need be careful: Standard LIS DP with Fenwick usually updates at value a with dp[a] = max(dp[a], query(a-1)+1), and answer query(X) = query(X). Here coordinate compression includes query X. Yes.

Now, is there any issue with using Fenwick prefix max when updating dp_end at p with a value that is not necessarily greater than current tree nodes? Fenwick update for max:
while i <= M:
    if val > bit[i]: bit[i] = val
    i += i & -i
This ensures query prefix includes this point update. Standard.

Now, let's prove answer = prefix max dp_end:
For any increasing subsequence with all values <=X, let its last value be v<=X. Its length L is at most dp_end[v] (max length ending with value v), so L <= max_{v<=X} dp_end[v]. Conversely, for any v<=X, a subsequence achieving dp_end[v] has all values <=v<=X (strictly increasing ending at v), so valid. Thus equality.

Now, update dp_end:
When processing new element a, any increasing subsequence ending at a consists of a plus an increasing subsequence ending at some value <a (or empty). The best previous length is max_{v<a} dp_end[v]. So new candidate length = best+1. Update dp_end[a] with max. This is standard.

This solution is O((N+Q) log M) with Fenwick. Very robust.

Let's consider if we need to process queries offline by R. Since dp_end evolves as we process positions. We can bucket queries by R. For each position i, update dp_end with A_i. Then answer queries with R=i by prefix max at X. This is correct because dp_end after processing first i positions.

Could we answer queries online if R sorted? Offline buckets.

Implementation details:
- Read all input.
- Build vals = sorted(set(A + Xs)).
- M = len(vals)
- bit = [0]*(M+1)
- Functions:
  def update(i, val): # i 1-based
    while i <= M:
        if val > bit[i]: bit[i] = val
        i += i & -i
  def query(i): # i 1-based, max over [1..i]
    res=0
    while i>0:
        if bit[i] > res: res = bit[i]
        i -= i & -i
    return res
- Precompute posA = [bisect_left(vals, a) + 1? Let's decide.
  Use 0-based p0 for A; update at p0+1.
  best = query(p0) because p0 is count of values <a. If p0=0 query0=0.
  update(p0+1, best+1)
  For query X, px0 = bisect_left(vals, X); ans=query(px0+1).

Need ensure p0 = bisect_left(vals, a) returns index of a. Since a in vals. Good.

- Buckets:
  buckets = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]
  For each query index qi, R, X:
    px0 = bisect_left(vals, X)
    buckets[R].append((px0, qi))
  Then loop i from 1 to N:
    p0 = posA[i-1]
    best = query(p0)
    update(p0+1, best+1)
    for px0, qi in buckets[i]:
        ans[qi] = query(px0+1)
  Output ans.

Now, consider if dp_end at a value can be updated with a subsequence that uses a later element with same value? No, update happens when processing that element; dp_end[a] may be from earlier occurrence. If later occurrence with same value has smaller best, max keeps earlier. If larger, updates. This is correct because ending value a can be any occurrence; using later occurrence with larger length is valid if previous indices before later occurrence. Since dp_end[a] from earlier occurrence might have used elements before earlier occurrence, which are also before later occurrence? Wait, if earlier occurrence at index i, later occurrence at index j>i. A subsequence ending at earlier occurrence has indices <=i, so all before j, so it is also a valid subsequence ending at value a but not necessarily using the later occurrence. For dp_end[a] as "ending with value a" in prefix up to current j, a subsequence ending at earlier occurrence is valid. If we want to extend from value a later, we need a subsequence ending at some occurrence of a before the next element. dp_end[a] stores max length of any subsequence ending with value a in the processed prefix. Since all processed indices are before future elements, any such subsequence can be extended by future elements. So keeping max is correct. If later occurrence yields longer, update. Good.

Let's design code:

import sys
from bisect import bisect_left

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N, Q = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    Rs = [0]*Q
    Xs = [0]*Q
    for i in range(Q):
        R, X = map(int, input().split())
        Rs[i] = R
        Xs[i] = X

    vals = sorted(set(A + Xs))
    M = len(vals)

    bit = [0]*(M+1)

    def update(i, v):
        while i <= M:
            if v > bit[i]:
                bit[i] = v
            i += i & -i

    def query(i):
        res = 0
        while i > 0:
            if bit[i] > res:
                res = bit[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return res

    buckets = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]
    for qi in range(Q):
        px = bisect_left(vals, Xs[qi]) + 1  # 1-based index for query
        buckets[Rs[qi]].append((px, qi))

    ans = [0]*Q
    for i, a in enumerate(A, 1):
        p0 = bisect_left(vals, a)  # 0-based index of a
        best = query(p0)  # max over values < a (1..p0)
        update(p0 + 1, best + 1)
        for px, qi in buckets[i]:
            ans[qi] = query(px)

    sys.stdout.write('\n'.join(map(str, ans)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let's verify off-by-one:
- p0 = bisect_left(vals, a). Since a in vals, p0 is 0-based index. Values <a are indices 0..p0-1, count p0. Fenwick query(i) returns max over 1..i. So query(p0) returns max over 1..p0, which corresponds to 0..p0-1. Correct.
- update(p0+1) updates 1-based index of a. Correct.
- For query X, px = bisect_left(vals, X)+1. Since X in vals, 1-based index. query(px) returns max over 1..px, values <=X. Correct.

Let's attempt to prove Fenwick DP invariant:
Let `bit` maintain for each compressed value index j (1-based) the maximum length of an increasing subsequence in the processed prefix whose last value is exactly vals[j-1]. More precisely, after processing first i elements, `bit` supports prefix max query over these dp_end values.
When processing a=vals[p0], the best previous length is max over values <a, which is `query(p0)`. Then a new subsequence of length best+1 ending at a exists. We update dp_end[a] to max(current, best+1) via `update(p0+1, best+1)`.
For a query threshold X=vals[px-1], the answer is max over values <=X of dp_end, which is `query(px)`.
This is standard and correct.

Now, let's think about if dp_end values should be updated with `best+1` even if best=0 and no previous subsequence; length1. Good.

Let's write final code with comments maybe:
```python
import sys
from bisect import bisect_left

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N, Q = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    Rs = [0] * Q
    Xs = [0] * Q
    for i in range(Q):
        R, X = map(int, input().split())
        Rs[i] = R
        Xs[i] = X

    vals_set = set(A)
    vals_set.update(Xs)
    vals = sorted(vals_set)
    M = len(vals)

    bit = [0] * (M + 1)

    def update(i, v):
        while i <= M:
            if v > bit[i]:
                bit[i] = v
            i += i & -i

    def query(i):
        res = 0
        while i > 0:
            if bit[i] > res:
                res = bit[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return res

    shift = Q.bit_length()
    mask = (1 << shift) - 1
    buckets = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    for qi in range(Q):
        px = bisect_left(vals, Xs[qi]) + 1
        buckets[Rs[qi]].append((px << shift) | qi)

    ans = [0] * Q
    for i, a in enumerate(A, 1):
        p0 = bisect_left(vals, a)
        best = query(p0)
        update(p0 + 1, best + 1)
        for code in buckets[i]:
            qi = code & mask
            px = code >> shift
            ans[qi] = query(px)

    sys.stdout.write('\n'.join(map(str, ans)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
Need ensure `mask` if shift=0? Q>=1 so shift>=1. Good.

Let's write final code with sorting approach:

```python
import sys
from bisect import bisect_left

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N, Q = map(int, input().split())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    R = [0] * Q
    X = [0] * Q
    for i in range(Q):
        r, x = map(int, input().split())
        R[i] = r
        X[i] = x

    vals_set = set(A)
    vals_set.update(X)
    vals = sorted(vals_set)
    del vals_set

    M = len(vals)
    bit = [0] * (M + 1)

    def add(i, v):
        while i <= M:
            if v > bit[i]:
                bit[i] = v
            i += i & -i

    def max_prefix(i):
        res = 0
        while i > 0:
            if bit[i] > res:
                res = bit[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return res

    order = list(range(Q))
    order.sort(key=R.__getitem__)

    ans = [0] * Q
    j = 0
    for idx, a in enumerate(A, 1):
        p = bisect_left(vals, a)  # 0-based index of a
        best = max_prefix(p)      # values < a
        add(p + 1, best + 1)

        while j < Q and R[order[j]] == idx:
            qi = order[j]
            px = bisect_left(vals, X[qi]) + 1  # 1-based index of X
            ans[qi] = max_prefix(px)
            j += 1

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(map(str, ans)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with `bit` max prefix query after point update max. Standard Fenwick for prefix max:
- update at i: while i<=M: bit[i]=max(bit[i],v); i += i&-i.
- query(i): res=0; while i>0: res=max(res, bit[i]); i -= i&-i.
This returns max over all point updates at indices <=i. Yes.

Let's prove with example: update at 2 with 5. add: i=2 bit[2]=5, i=4 bit[4]=5, i=8... query(3): i=3 res=bit[3]=0, i=2 res=max(0,bit[2]=5)=5, i=0. query(1): i=1 bit[1]=0. Good. update at 1 with 3. add: bit[1]=3, bit[2]=max(5,3)=5, bit[4]=5. query(1)=3, query(2)=max(bit[2]=5, bit[1]=3)=5. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with if `bit` tree values are not monotonic in the Fenwick sense? Fenwick for prefix max with point updates works even if point values arbitrary; query accumulates max over O(log) nodes. Good.

Let's prove Fenwick prefix max with point updates:
- update at i: for each tree node j that covers i, set bit[j]=max(bit[j],v).
- query(i): visits nodes whose ranges partition [1..i], taking max. Since every point update at k<=i has updated at least one visited node, and no point update at k>i updates any visited node? Actually a point update at k>i might update a node that is also visited by query(i)? Let's check standard Fenwick: query(i) visits indices i, i - lowbit(i), ... These nodes are exactly those whose ranges are subsets of [1..i]. An update at k>i updates nodes k, k+lowbit(k), ... Could one of these be in query(i) path? Suppose k>i, update at k updates node k (which is >i), not in query path because query path indices <=i. It may update node m>k>i, not in query path. So no. Thus query returns max over updates with k<=i. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with if `max_prefix(p)` for p = count of values <a returns max over dp_end for values with index <=p (1-based), which are values with 0-based index <p. Since p is count, yes. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with if `p = bisect_left(vals, a)` and vals contains query values less than a; p includes them. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with if `vals_set.update(X)` includes query X values that are less than some A; p for that A includes them. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with if query X is less than all A values but guarantee prevents? If X in vals and less than all A, px maybe 0? Wait if X is less than all A, but X in vals, then vals[0] = X, and A values >X. p for min A would be 1 (because X before it), best query1 includes dp_end at X (0). Good. Query X px=1 max_prefix1=0 if no processed element <=X. But guarantee says X>=min prefix, so if X less than all A in prefix, impossible. But if prefix has no element <=X, answer0. Our algorithm would output0. Good.

Fenwick functions:
def add(i, v, bit=bit, M=M):
    while i <= M:
        if v > bit[i]:
            bit[i] = v
        i += i & -i
def query(i, bit=bit):
    res = 0
    while i:
        if bit[i] > res:
            res = bit[i]
        i -= i & -i
    return res
Default arguments localize bit/M, faster. We can define:
    def add(i, v):
        while i <= M:
...
Local variable lookup for M and bit is closure (cell) slower than local. We can bind `bit_local = bit`, `M_local = M` inside function? Default args:
    def add(i, v, bit=bit, M=M):
        ...
    def query(i, bit=bit):
        ...
This makes bit and M local. Good. Use this.

In main loop, function call overhead remains. Could inline query/update in loop for speed but code less clean. 6e5 calls okay. But if time tight, default args help.

Let's write:
    def add(i, v, bit=bit, M=M):
        while i <= M:
            if v > bit[i]:
                bit[i] = v
            i += i & -i

    def prefix_max(i, bit=bit):
        res = 0
        while i > 0:
            if bit[i] > res:
                res = bit[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return res

This is good.

Could also avoid `bisect_left` for A by using a dictionary `pos = {v:i for i,v in enumerate(vals)}` and then `p = pos[a]`. Dict lookup O(1) but Python dict lookup maybe faster than C bisect? Actually bisect_left is C but does log M Python comparisons; dict lookup is C hash. For 2e5, both fine. Dict memory extra ~ (M entries) maybe 20MB. Not needed.

Could precompute `posA = [bisect_left(vals, a) for a in A]` to avoid bisect in loop? It would use memory but maybe faster? Bisect in loop is C; list comprehension also C? It calls bisect. Not needed.

Could precompute `posX` for queries to avoid bisect at answer time? We can compute when reading? Need vals. Could after vals:
    posX = [0]*Q
    for qi in range(Q): posX[qi] = bisect_left(vals, X[qi]) + 1
Then in processing, use posX[qi]. This adds memory Q ints ~7.2 but avoids Q bisects during processing. But bisects are C, not bottleneck. Not needed.
